# Cornelis de Heem: Study Notes

**Site:** Cornelis de Heem (bapt. 8 April 1631 - buried 17 May 1695), Dutch/Flemish still-life painter; the son of Jan Davidsz. de Heem, trained in his father's Antwerp studio, member of the Antwerp Guild of Saint Luke in 1660, who painted small fruit pieces in strong blues that drift away from his father's painterliness -- and the second generation of the de Heem workshop line, whose hand is still being separated from his father's, his brother's and his son's. Some eighty pictures survive in the Commons category alone, from the green-based 1654 basket to the tall 1695 niche heap, and the whole of his manner is a narrowing: the same abundance his father painted large, warm and painterly, folded small, blue and hard.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Clara Peeters study: from the woman who put her own face in the reflection of a gilt cup and shaped the breakfast piece at its source, to the son who was trained in his father's Antwerp studio, became a member of the Antwerp Guild in 1660, and painted the fruit piece small, blue and hard.

One-hundred-and-eighty-first doorway check in a row. The legs disagree on this check, and the disagreement is recorded here rather than smoothed over. Leg 1 records it as a NON-HOLD on the substance and a NON-HOLD on the letter -- the fifty-fifth non-hold; the substance-hold count stays at 132. Leg 3 records it as a HOLD on the substance and a NON-HOLD on the letter -- the fifty-fifth non-hold; the substance-hold count stays at 132. The parent follows leg 1 on the substance (D2, D6) and on D3 and D7 in the letter, for the reasons given under each claim below; leg 3 agrees with leg 1 on the D2, D4 and D6 letter fails. The check is recorded as a NON-HOLD on the substance and a NON-HOLD on the letter -- the fifty-fifth non-hold; the substance-hold count stays at 132 -- and the streak that began at #420 now stands at one hundred and thirty-two holds and fifty-five non-holds. The substance fails on D2 and D6; the letter fails on D2, D3, D4, D6 and D7. D1 and D5 hold in substance and in letter. (The tally arithmetic is flagged, not fixed: 132 + 55 = 187 against 181 checks run -- see C729. The parent carries the log's own tally and records the excess honestly.)

D1 "Cornelis de Heem (bapt. 8 April 1631 - buried 17 May 1695)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (both legs agree). EN Wikipedia Cornelis de Heem, L2: "Cornelis de Heem (8 April 1631 (baptized) - 17 May 1695 (buried))" -- exact, digit for digit. Corroborated in #557 leg 3 N7.

D2 "the son who inherited the workshop his father built on that table, trained by Jan Davidsz. de Heem, Guild master in 1660" -- FAILS in substance (leg 1; leg 3 holds it in substance and the parent does not follow it) and FAILS in the letter (both legs agree). Two of the three elements hold. He was his father's son (Wikipedia L3-4) and trained by him: Sotheby's lot 181, "In his formative years Cornelis de Heem was trained in the studio of his father Jan Davidsz. de Heem (1606-1683/4) in Antwerp"; Meijer via #557 leg 1 F36: "without doubt trained" by his father; Wikipedia hedges only slightly, "He appears to have been trained by his father in Antwerp". The Guild year holds in substance: Sotheby's, "admitted to the Guild of Saint Luke in Antwerp as a painter in his own right" in 1660-61; Ketterer, "In 1660 the Antwerp painters' gild of St. Luke accepted Cornelis de Heem as independent painter"; Wikipedia, "He became a member of the Antwerp painters' guild in 1660". But the load-bearing element fails in substance: no source read this leg or cited in #557 says Cornelis inherited or took over any workshop. The phrase was coined inside the chain, in #557 leg 3 (L306: "the son who inherited the workshop"), and carried twice. Against it: the father's Utrecht workshop went to Abraham Mignon in 1672 (#557 leg 1 F34: Mignon "remained there after de Heem's departure for Antwerp in 1672, taking over the workshop that year"), and Cornelis left Antwerp for the north in 1667 and worked as a self-employed painter "much more subject to market conditions than it was the case in his father's studio" (Ketterer). The letter fails as well: "inherited the workshop" has no source wording anywhere, and "Guild master" overstates what the sources say -- Wikipedia says "member", Sotheby's and Ketterer say "as a painter in his own right" / "independent painter", and no source read calls him master in 1660 (leg 1 C709; leg 3 C742, C743). Leg 3's dissent on the substance: son, training and Guild all hold, so the claim holds in substance and fails only in the letter. The parent does not follow it: the claim as phrased is about the son who inherited the workshop, and the inheritance is the claim. A claim whose defining clause is undocumented and contradicted is not true in substance because its other clauses hold. The defensible form, used throughout this study, is "trained in his father's Antwerp studio, Guild member 1660, then self-employed in the north".

D3 "painting small jewel-like fruit pieces in strong blues that drift away from his father's painterliness" -- HOLDS in substance, FAILS in the letter (leg 1; leg 3 holds it in letter too, and the parent follows leg 1). The substance holds: Wikipedia L11-12, "Cornelis's works, however, tend to be small, display a preference for strong blues, and, over time, shifted away from the painterly style preferred by his father"; Ketterer, "His still lives, mostly executed on canvas, were made in smaller format... Cornelis de Heem was more dedicated to still lives of fruits... all made in strong colors". The drift is real as a mechanism: Ketterer, "With realistic details, Cornelis de Heem attains a surprisingly easy and swift stroke of the brush, a feature that became more manifest as of the 1670s" (leg 1 F11). The letter fails on one word: "jewel-like" appears in no source read; it is #557 leg 3's gloss (L307: "small jewel-like fruit pieces"). "Drift away" for source "shifted away" is a fair synonym, and "fruit pieces" is supported; only the single adjective is invented, but the letter is the letter (leg 1 C713). Leg 3's dissent: the claim is near-verbatim from Wikipedia, and "jewel-like" is the only added epithet. The parent follows leg 1: the series has always failed letter claims that add an unsourced epithet to a sourced sentence, and "jewel-like" is the word the prompts would build on, so it matters.

D4 "the chain has spent two studies on the de Heem line's source (#556 van der Ast, the teacher in the Utrecht studio; #557 de Heem, the pupil who made the bouquet into a banquet); Cornelis carries that line into its second generation" -- HOLDS in substance, FAILS in the letter (both legs agree). #556 (log header L5166; study file balthasar-van-der-ast.md) and #557 (log header L5174; study file jan-davidsz-de-heem.md) are real studies; Cornelis is Jan's son and literally the second generation (Wikipedia L3, L6; #557 leg 1 F16: three sons became painters, Cornelis from the first marriage); "the pupil who made the bouquet into a banquet" is the chain's own framing, attested in the log (L5172, L5180). The letter fails narrowly: "the teacher in the Utrecht studio" states as definite what every source hedges -- #557 leg 1 D6, "'Probable teacher' only -- no document" (NGA: early works "resemble those of... Balthasar van der Ast..., who may have been his teacher"); #557 leg 1 F5, "then (probably) under Balthasar van der Ast in Utrecht"; #556 study file L224, "(probably) Jan Davidsz. de Heem" (leg 1 C714; leg 3 C744). Getty ULAN lists van der Ast as de Heem's teacher, but the flat form is not supported.

D5 "Why Cornelis over Nicolaes van Verendael and Elias van den Broeck... Verendael is the collaborator and Broeck the pupil who carried the pronk still life back to Amsterdam" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (both legs agree). Verendael the collaborator: Wikipedia Verendael, "He was a frequent collaborator of other Antwerp artists to whose compositions he added the still life elements"; "He was in demand as a collaborator working with some of the leading Antwerp painters such as David Teniers the Younger, Gonzales Coques, Erasmus Quellinus II, Jan Boeckhorst, Carstian Luyckx and Jan Davidsz. de Heem" (leg 1 P4; leg 3 D5). Broeck the pupil who returned to Amsterdam: Wikipedia Broeck, "in 1669 he became a pupil of Jan Davidsz. de Heem in Utrecht... In 1673 he accompanied de Heem to Antwerp, where he became a master in the Guild of St. Luke there"; "In 1685 he returned to Amsterdam"; "His banquet still lifes were influenced by his second master Jan Davidsz. de Heem". Broeck was Amsterdam-born, so "back" is right. "The collaborator" and "the pupil who carried the pronk still life back to Amsterdam" are fair compressed paraphrases of the sourced facts.

D6 "Cornelis is the documented one: dated, a Guild master, a workshop inherited rather than borrowed" -- FAILS in substance (leg 1; leg 3 holds it in substance and the parent does not follow it) and FAILS in the letter (both legs agree). Dated holds: first known dated works 1654 (#557 leg 1 F36; Ketterer, "dated works as of 1654"); Still-life with a wine glass and oysters, signed and dated 1659, Musee Fabre (Sotheby's); NMWA Still Life with a Basket of Fruit, c. 1654, signed "C. DHEEM. f.". Guild holds (see D2). But "a workshop inherited rather than borrowed" fails: undocumented (see D2 / leg 1 C709), and the contrast with Verendael and Broeck depends entirely on it. The letter fails: no source says "inherited" (or "borrowed") about any workshop; the wording is #557 leg 3's coinage (L306-308), carried into #559. Leg 3's dissent on the substance is the same as D2 and the parent's answer is the same: the inheritance is the claim.

Leg 1's and leg 2's pages tables likewise list no Sotheby's page read end to end. The 46-line count belongs to Wikipedia alone. ## The life

Cornelis de Heem was baptised in Leiden on 8 April 1631 and buried in Antwerp on 17 May 1695, aged 64 (leg 1 F1). He was the son of Jan Davidsz. de Heem (1606-1684), "the most significant" member of "a large family of still-life specialists" (Wikipedia L2-4), and he moved with his family from Leiden to Antwerp in 1636, aged five (leg 1 F3). He was trained by his father in Antwerp -- Sotheby's does not hedge ("In his formative years Cornelis de Heem was trained in the studio of his father Jan Davidsz. de Heem (1606-1683/4) in Antwerp"), Meijer via #557 leg 1 F36 says he was "without doubt trained" by his father, and at 23 (1654) he produced his first known dated works as a "skilful and fully trained still-life painter"; Wikipedia hedges only slightly ("He appears to have been trained by his father in Antwerp") and the NMWA says "he probably studied under his father" (leg 1 F4). He became a member of the Antwerp painters' guild in 1660 (Wikipedia L8-9); Sotheby's has him "admitted to the Guild of Saint Luke in Antwerp as a painter in his own right" in 1660-61, and Ketterer has the guild accepting him "as independent painter" in 1660 (leg 1 F5). He is a Guild member in 1660, not a Guild master: no source read calls him master in 1660, and the study does not (leg 3 C742).

After Antwerp the track runs north. There is proof for his activities with his father, who had meanwhile returned to Utrecht, for the second half of 1667; the track is then lost until 1676, when he is named as husband of Maria van Beusecom in IJsselstein near Utrecht; then The Hague, "where he stayed until at least 1684"; he returned to Antwerp in 1691/92 and died there in 1695 (Ketterer; leg 1 F6). Wikipedia's compressed form is "from 1667 until the late 1680s he was variously active in Utrecht, IJsselstein, and The Hague". The Dyrham Park picture was painted c. 1685 "when he was living in The Hague" (Art Fund).

What he did NOT do is inherit a workshop. No source read this leg or cited in #557 says Cornelis inherited or took over any workshop. The father's Utrecht workshop went to Abraham Mignon in 1672, and Cornelis worked as a self-employed painter in the north from 1667, "much more subject to market conditions than it was the case in his father's studio" (Ketterer). The "inherited the workshop" phrase was coined in #557 leg 3 and carried twice through #558 and #559; this study strikes it and does not repeat it as fact (leg 1 C709). The family business is real -- father, two sons, a workshop, and the question of whose hand is whose -- but the workshop was the father's, and the son left it.

The attribution tangle is the family itself. Wikipedia: the family included "his brother Jan Jansz., nephew Jan Jansz. II, and son David Cornelisz. (1663 - after 1718), who all painted mostly flower and fruit pieces in a similar style and probably often collaborated"; "It is often not easy to distinguish the works of the different members of the family" (leg 1 F7). Jan Davidsz. had nine children; three sons became painters: David Janszoon (b. 29 November 1628) and Cornelis from the first marriage, Jan (b. 1650) from the second; Cornelis's son David Corneliszoon (1663-1718) also painted (#557 leg 1 F16). Houbraken's claim that the father retouched his sons' and Mignon's work has no evidence in Cornelis's work per Meijer, and Jan Jansz. as a painter is "most probably a myth" (#557 leg 1 C8). David Corneliszoon's death date conflicts: "after 1718" (Wikipedia L10) vs "1663-1701" in the article's own family tree (L18-19) -- carried as a flag, not resolved (leg 1 C715; leg 3 C748 on the Corneliszoon/Cornelisz spelling split: the log uses "Corneliszoon", "Cornelisz" has 0 log occurrences; this study uses Corneliszoon and notes the other spelling).

Dated works run from 1654: first known dated works 1654 (#557 leg 1 F36; Ketterer, "dated works as of 1654"); Still Life with a Basket of Fruit, c. 1654, oil on panel, 44.5 x 72.5 cm, signed lower left "C. DHEEM. f.", National Museum of Western Art, Tokyo; Still-life with a wine glass and oysters, signed and dated 1659, Musee Fabre, Montpellier (Sotheby's); Pageantry Still Life with copulating sparrows, 1657, Stadelsches Kunstinstitut (leg 1 F8). The Mauritshuis Fruit Still Life dated 1695 in a low-quality source only is NOT verified (leg 1 C723; leg 2 correction 4).

Scale is a habit, not a limitation. "Cornelis's works, however, tend to be small" (Wikipedia L11). Ketterer: "His still lives, mostly executed on canvas, were made in smaller format and seem a bit more coarse in their execution" than his father's. NOMA's Still Life with Fruit on a Ledge is 26 1/2 x 21 1/2 in.; Sotheby's lot 181 is 48 x 64 cm. The exception is the Dyrham Park Still Life of Flowers and Fruit Arranged on a Stone Plinth in a Garden, c. 1685, 92 x 77 cm, "unusual in its large scale and outdoor setting" (Art Fund; leg 1 F9). No oeuvre total was established this leg: Wikimedia Commons lists 78 files in Category:Paintings by Cornelis de Heem (search result, not inspected), and no catalogue raisonne count for Cornelis was found in the sources read; Meijer's 2016 thesis catalogues the father, not the son (leg 1 F10). No technical study (pigments, ground, underdrawing) was found for Cornelis specifically; the blue-blooming note (M5) is a condition report, not a technical study (leg 1, section 5).

His standing in the sources is high and hedged at once. Sotheby's (cited in #557 leg 3 N7 from search, carried, NOT re-verified end to end this leg): "considered one of the most accomplished still-life painters of the Dutch Golden Age". Ketterer, on the young Antwerp work: "of a remarkable quality, not at all inferior to his father's works" (leg 1 F21).

## M1. The peeled-lemon spiral

What it IS: a lemon half-peeled, the peel unwound in a long ribbon that curls down and often hangs over the front edge of the ledge. In W20 the peel ribbon runs from the half-peeled orange down across the ledge; in W11 the peel loops down over the table edge in a full circle; in W7 the peel uncoils in a long spiral past the ledge; in W14 and W19 the peel ribbon lies beside the fruit; in W2 it curls at the centre of the heap. The peel is almost always the brightest yellow in the picture, and it is the longest continuous line in the composition: it starts at the cut face of the fruit and ends in empty air below the ledge. It does three jobs: it shows the cut (the fruit has been opened), it leads the eye out of the picture and over the edge, and it is the one object that is neither fruit nor vessel but the skin of the fruit, turned into a ribbon. It appears in the earliest work (W1, the peeled lemon in the NMWA diagonal) and the latest (W20, 1695), and in at least ten of the twenty-two works seen. Ground: leg 2 M1.

**Takeaway:** the lemon is never whole and never just cut: it is peeled, and the peel is a yellow ribbon that falls out of the picture.

## M2. The overhanging spill

What it IS: fruit, peel, wheat and cherries cascade over the front edge of the stone ledge and hang in the air below it. In the Warsaw apples-and-grapes (Beyond) a bunch of white grapes pours over the edge and cherries dangle on their stems below the ledge; in W8 a bunch of grapes cascades over the ledge and physalis dangle beneath it; in W2 cherries dangle below the ledge; in W19 a peach hangs over the edge on its stem; in W10 cherries and wheat spill below the ledge; in W13 the lemon peel hangs in a long loop past the table edge. The NOMA object lesson states the mechanism plainly: "Orange peels and wheat stalks spill over the ledge, lessening the gap between the pictorial space and the viewer's space." The spill is not decoration: it is the device that makes the ledge a real edge, with air below it, and the viewer standing in front of it. It appears in the early work (W1, fruit spilling from the tipped basket) and grows more deliberate over time: the early spill is a heap sliding off a basket, the late spill is a single bunch or a single peel, placed. The same device in the NMWA picture: the tipped basket spills fruit along the diagonal toward the viewer (leg 1 F15). Ground: leg 2 M2; leg 1 F15.

**Takeaway:** something always falls off the front of the table: grapes, cherries, peel, wheat. The spill is what makes the ledge an edge you stand at.

## M3. The stone ledge in a niche

What it IS: the fruit sits on a stone slab inside a dark arched niche, the arch visible at the top corners, the stone's edge a hard horizontal line across the picture. In W20 the niche is a tall dark arch and the ledge is a stone block; in W8 the arch frames the heap like a shrine; in W7 the ledge sits inside a dark arch with the fruit piled on it; in W17 the bouquet stands in a stone niche outdoors. The niche does two things: it gives the heap a frame smaller than the picture, so the fruit is a display inside a display, and it makes the dark ground architectural -- the black is not a void but a wall with an opening in it. The ledge itself is almost always grey stone, its front face visible as a band, and the signature is often carved into it (M12). The niche appears in the middle and late works, not the early garden pieces: the early pictures open onto landscape (W3, W5), the later ones close into the niche. The pile itself is one interlocked mass: NOMA, "The complex overlapping arrangement and consistent tonal quality produce an emphatically unified composition" (leg 1 F14) -- the pile is not a catalogue of objects but a single tonal key held together. Ground: leg 2 M3; leg 1 F14.

**Takeaway:** the table becomes a stone shelf in a dark arch: the fruit is a display in a cabinet, not a meal on a table.

## M4. The grape cluster as light-catcher

What it IS: bunches of white or pale green grapes, each grape a separate translucent sphere with its own small white highlight, painted against the black ground so they glow. In W19 two bunches, green and pale gold, are the whole picture, each grape lit from a different side; in W9 a huge bunch of white grapes stands behind the lobster, the pale mass answering the red; in W6 white grapes spill from the porcelain bowl; in the f24 still life (Beyond) white grapes at left balance the dark plums at right. The grapes are the light source of the picture in the way a lamp would be: they are the palest, brightest thing, and they sit in the middle of the heap so the light seems to come from inside the fruit. Red grapes do the same work in a lower key (W11, W14), but the white grape is the signature: green-white, translucent, each sphere separate, the bunch a cluster of small moons. It is there in every period, from W1 (grapes in the NMWA diagonal) to W20 (green and dark grapes in the 1695 heap). Ground: leg 2 M4.

**Takeaway:** the grapes are the lamp: white, translucent, each with its own highlight, glowing out of the black.

## M5. The strong blues

What it IS: saturated blue used as a deliberate accent against the warm fruit and the black ground. Wikipedia states it as the diagnostic: his works "display a preference for strong blues". In W14 the whole table is draped in a strong blue cloth with a gold fringe, the lower half of the picture blue; in W15 and W16 the hanging fruit is tied with a big blue ribbon bow; in W9 and W11 the table is draped in dark blue; in W12 the pocket watch hangs on a blue ribbon; in W6 and the f24 still life (Beyond) the porcelain bowl is blue-and-white; in W10 and W17 blue morning glories punctuate the heap. The blue is never the ground and never the fruit: it is the cloth, the ribbon, the porcelain, the flower -- the manufactured or the small thing that carries the colour. Against the red lobster (W14), the orange (W15) and the black ground, the blue is the cold note that keeps the warm heap from going muddy. It is also what separates him from his father: the father's pictures are warmer and more painterly; the son's are cooler, bluer, harder. The blues are literal and fugitive: Sotheby's condition note on lot 181, "The blue of the plums and the porcelain bowl have bloomed a little" -- the blue pigment has bloomed under the varnish, a conservation fact that is also a visual fact (soft, milky blue over dark plums and Delftware) (leg 1 F12). And the blue is not the whole palette: the early NMWA Basket of Fruit (c. 1654) is "green-based, with accents found in the yellow lemon and the red cherries", and Ketterer has "lighter colors dominating the early period of creation and darker tones at a later point" -- the strong-blue, dark-ground Cornelis is the mature one; the early one is lighter and greener (leg 1 F13). Ground: leg 2 M5; leg 1 F12, F13.

**Takeaway:** blue is the cold note: the cloth, the ribbon, the porcelain, the morning glory. The father's pictures are warm; the son's are blue.

## M6. The red lobster as colour anchor

What it IS: a whole cooked lobster, saturated red, laid across the foreground of the picture as the single largest patch of strong colour. In W9 the lobster lies across the lower half on a pewter plate, the only red against black glass and green grapes; in W6 the lobster lies at lower right, its claws toward the viewer; in W14 the lobster lies on the blue cloth, red on blue, the strongest colour contrast in the corpus; in W15 a single red lobster claw hangs at the edge of the festoon. The lobster does the job a red cloth does in other painters: it anchors the composition, gives the eye a place to land, and sets the scale -- everything else is measured against it. It is also the most sculptural object in the corpus: segmented, jointed, its claws and antennae breaking the outline of the heap. It appears in the 1660s (W6, 1660-1670) and stays; it never appears in the early garden pieces. Ground: leg 2 M6.

**Takeaway:** the lobster is the red anchor: one cooked red animal across the foreground, the biggest patch of colour in the picture, everything else measured against it.

## M7. The silver vessel and the material catalogue

What it IS: each picture is a set of rendering problems, one per material, solved side by side. Silver: the chased vessel with the pierced domed lid in W11, the silver caster in W12, the tazza in W4, the chalice in the putti piece (Beyond) -- reflective, engraved, warm grey. Pewter: the dull plates under the lobster (W9) and the oysters (W11, W12). Glass: the dark roemer in W9, nearly black, legible only by its rim; the huge facon de Venise glass in W14, transparent with the wine visible; the dark glass vase in W18, the stems visible through it. Porcelain: the blue-and-white bowl in W6 and the f24 still life, hard and thin. Gold: the small ewer in W13. The materials are named and specific, not generic: the silver is chased, the glass is Venetian, the porcelain is Chinese. The catalogue is tighter than his father's: fewer kinds of vessel, repeated more often, each one rendered harder and more exactly. The father's painterliness is the thing that goes away; the catalogue stays and sharpens. The same reuse runs through the motifs: Sotheby's, the facon de Venise wine glass filled with blackberry sprigs, the Delft bowl and the distinctive curved stone arch behind "may be found in another signed canvas" -- identical motifs recur across pictures years apart, the workshop reusing props, the picture a new arrangement of a known stock (leg 1 F17). And the handling itself changes: Ketterer, "With realistic details, Cornelis de Heem attains a surprisingly easy and swift stroke of the brush, a feature that became more manifest as of the 1670s" -- after leaving his father's studio his work became "generally less elaborate and less meticulously finished" (Meijer via #557 leg 1 F36). Early pictures are tight and father-close; late pictures are looser, swifter, darker (leg 1 F11). Ground: leg 2 M7; leg 1 F11, F17.

**Takeaway:** the picture is a shelf of materials, each in its own touch: silver chased, pewter dull, glass clear, porcelain hard. The son renders them harder than the father.

## M8. The living intruder

What it IS: one or more small living things in a picture of cut fruit and dead shellfish, always at an edge, always small, always doing something. The copulating sparrows in W4, on a branch in the middle of the pronk. The red admiral butterfly in W4, W7 and W16. The snail on the ledge in W18 and W20, on the vine in W10, on the twig in W6. The white moth on the peach in W20 and among the garland in W16. The lizard and the slug at the foot of the bouquet in W17. The frogs of the Bavarian still life (Beyond, title only -- not clearly visible in the black-and-white reproduction). The fly on the putto's back (Beyond) and at the edge of W7. The spider at the edge of W18. The intruder is never in the centre and never large except the sparrows; it is placed at an edge or at the bottom, and it changes the picture's tense: everything else is cut, picked or cooked, the intruder is alive and moving. It also gives the scale: the snail next to a peach, the butterfly next to a rose, the fly on a back. It appears in every period, from the 1657 sparrows to the 1695 snail and moth. Ground: leg 2 M8.

**Takeaway:** something small in the picture is alive: a snail on the ledge, a butterfly on the rose, sparrows on the branch. The dead things are the setting; the living thing is the event.

## M9. The split and cut fruit

What it IS: fruit cut open to show the inside -- flesh, seeds, stone -- always at the centre of the heap, always the lightest or reddest thing in it. The cut melon in W1 (NMWA: the basket sits tipped up on a melon "cut open to reveal its flesh"), W4 and W17; the split peach in W10 and W20; the split fig in W7; the cut pie in W13; the split pomegranate in W10. The cut does three things: it proves the fruit is ripe (the flesh is coloured and wet), it gives the picture its brightest interior (orange melon flesh, red fig, pale peach), and it starts the clock -- cut fruit rots, and the picture knows it. The NOMA reading makes the vanitas explicit: the fruit is "perfectly ripe", and therefore about to decay. The cut is never at the edge; it is at the centre, the picture's heart, and the uncut fruit surrounds it like a rind. Ground: leg 2 M9.

**Takeaway:** one fruit is always cut open at the centre: melon, peach, fig, pomegranate. The cut is the bright heart, and it is also the clock.

## M10. The hanging festoon and garland

What it IS: fruit and flowers tied with a blue ribbon and hung against the dark ground, with no table at all. In W15 a single festoon hangs from one big blue bow; in W16 a garland hangs from two bows, sagging in the middle; the Menil swag (Beyond, attributed) is a third. Gravity does the composition: the heaviest bunches hang lowest, the grapes in the middle, the peach at the top, the medlars at the bottom. The ribbon is the only manufactured object and the only thing holding the picture up: cut the ribbon and the fruit falls. The festoon is the most portable of his formulas -- it needs no table, no room, no landscape, just a nail and a bow -- and it is also the most decorative: the fruit is arranged by weight and by colour, not by use. It appears in the middle and late work, not the early garden pieces, and it is the formula that most clearly leaves the father's table behind. Ground: leg 2 M10.

**Takeaway:** tie the fruit to a blue ribbon and hang it: no table, no room, just gravity and a bow. Cut the ribbon and it all falls.

## M11. The garden and grotto background

What it IS: in the early works the still life is set in a place, not a void: a garden balustrade with a view (W5, 1658), a stone grotto with a landscape beyond (W3, c. 1657), a sky with clouds, a tree trunk and a brick wall outdoors (W17), a sky at top left (the frogs piece, Beyond). The background is architecture and nature, not black: a balustrade, a grotto, a garden stairway, a blue-and-white porcelain pitcher standing among the vegetables as a sign of wealth. The device comes from the father: Jan Davidsz.'s 1655 Hermitage fruit-and-flowers in a landscape supplies the celery, artichoke and melon of W3, and his 1653 Schleissheim fruit piece supplies the horizontal format. Over time the background closes: the middle works go black, the late works go into the niche (M3). The garden is the early manner, and its loss is the clearest single change in the corpus: from a place to a cabinet. The vanitas moves outside with it: the Art Fund Dyrham Park picture (c. 1685) is "a handsome example of the Dutch vanitas genre in which the wilting plants and decaying fruit symbolise the transitory nature of life", but it is outdoors -- flowers and fruit on a stone plinth in a garden, roses, lilies, peonies, peaches and a melon. The vanitas moves outside: decay in daylight, not on a dark ledge (leg 1 F18). Ground: leg 2 M11; leg 1 F18.

**Takeaway:** the early pictures happen somewhere: a garden, a grotto, a balustrade. Then the background closes to black, and the fruit moves into a niche.

## M12. The signature on the stone

What it IS: the name is written on the stone of the ledge or the table edge, as if carved into it, not painted onto the picture. "C. DE HEEM" on the ledge at lower left in W20; "C. DE. HEEM" on the stone at right in W17; "C. DE HEEM" on the table edge at lower left in W11; "C. DE HEEM" lower right in W16; "C. DHEEM. f." on the edge of the table in W1 (NMWA); "C. De Heem f" lower right in the Christie's niche piece (Beyond). The signature is always small, always at an edge, always on the stone or the wood of the furniture -- never floating on the dark ground, never on the fruit. It is the same family of placement as Peeters' engraved knife, one study earlier: the name belongs to the things in the picture, not to the picture. It appears from the earliest dated work (W1, c. 1654) to the latest (W20, 1695). Against it stands the family attribution problem (The life): unsigned or "J de Heem"-signed work slides between father, sons and nephew, and Meijer found no evidence for Houbraken's retouching anecdote in Cornelis's work (leg 1 F20). Ground: leg 2 M12; leg 1 F20.

**Takeaway:** he does not sign the painting; he carves his name into the ledge it sits on.

## M13. The jewel-like small scale

What it IS: the pictures are small, dense cabinet pieces, not wall-filling banquets. Wikipedia states it as the diagnostic, beside the blues: his works "tend to be small". The verified sizes: W1 44.5 x 72.5 cm, W2 53.3 x 73.8 cm. The reported sizes: W5 69.8 x 87.1 cm, W3 64.4 x 78 cm, W10 55 x 73 cm, W20 65 x 50 cm, the NOMA ledge 67.3 x 54.6 cm, the Menil swag 68.3 x 54.6 cm. Nothing in the corpus seen is over 92 cm in either dimension (the Art Fund plinth, c. 1685, at 92 x 77 cm, is the largest reported), and most are under 75 cm wide. The smallness is not a limitation: it is the format. The heap fills the panel edge to edge, the fruit is life-size or larger, and the viewer stands close, the way one stands close to a jewel box. The father's large banqueting pieces (the Hermitage 95 x 124.5 cm) are the contrast: the son takes the same abundance and folds it into a cabinet. Fruit is the centre and everything else is in support: Ketterer, "Cornelis de Heem was more dedicated to still lives of fruits, sometimes increased by flowers, food items (lobster, oysters, small loafs of bread) and table devices... among which there also were garlands, festoons and bouquets, were all made in strong colors" (leg 1 F19). Note on the word: "jewel-like" itself is the chain's gloss, not a source word (D3) -- the study keeps the mechanism and drops the adjective from its claims, using it only as the mechanism's name, where the smallness is sourced. Ground: leg 2 M13; leg 1 F9, F19.

**Takeaway:** the pictures are small and dense: a jewel box, not a banquet hall. The fruit is life-size, the panel is arm's length, the heap fills the frame.

## M14. The vanitas undertow

What it IS: the ripe fruit is always also a reminder that it will rot, and the picture always carries one explicit vanitas note beside the abundance. The NOMA object lesson gives the museum's reading in full: the lemon is "inviting on the outside, but sour on the inside", the oyster a symbol of lust, the butterfly the soul and its redemption, the grapes and wheat the Eucharist, and the perfectly ripe fruit a reminder of "the erosive character of time". In W4 the note is the mirror, the flute and the copulating sparrows; in W12 the pocket watch beside the oysters; in W17 the mushrooms, lizard and slug at the foot of the bouquet; in W9 the dark roemer, nearly invisible in the black; in W13 the tipped goblet; in the 1661 Vanitas Still Life with Musical Instruments (Beyond, text-only) the instruments, books and skulls are the subject itself. The undertow is quieter than in his father's work and quieter than in Peeters: there is no skull in any picture seen, no hourglass, no snuffed candle. The vanitas is carried by the fruit itself -- ripe, cut, and about to spoil -- and by the small living things that eat it. But the symbolic system is a mode he switches on, not a property of every picture: the NMWA says of the early Basket of Fruit that "such works often had vanitas meanings during that period, this work seems to not have any specifically symbolic meaning" (leg 1 F16). The symbolism is object by object -- lemon, oyster, butterfly, grapes, wheat -- and the same object can carry it in one picture and not in the next. Ground: leg 2 M14; leg 1 F16.

**Takeaway:** the fruit is perfectly ripe, which is the vanitas: it is about to rot. No skull needed; the cut peach is the clock, the snail is the eater, the butterfly is the soul.

## Works

Twenty numbered works, ordered early to late. Dimensions are VERIFIED only where a museum page was read end to end or a museum API record was retrieved; otherwise REPORTED (source named) or NOT VERIFIED. 2 of the 20 have verified dimensions; 4 more have reported dimensions; 14 have none. A third verified dimension set belongs to a text-only work in Beyond the 20 (NOMA).

## W1 -- Still Life with a Basket of Fruit, c. 1654 (TEXT ONLY)

Oil on panel, 44.5 x 72.5 cm, VERIFIED (National Museum of Western Art, Tokyo collection page, read end to end, P.1990-0002). Signed lower left on the edge of the table: "C. DHEEM. f." A wood table draped with a green cloth set parallel to a dark green wall; the left edge of the table forms a diagonal, and the array of fruit -- grapes, peaches, apricots, cherries and plums -- spills from a basket tipped to the upper left in the same diagonal direction; two walnuts form a diagonal with a peeled lemon, a bunch of grapes and grape leaves; the basket sits tipped up on a cut-open melon. The museum calls the overall palette green-based, with accents in the yellow lemon and the red cherries, and notes the work seems to have no specifically symbolic meaning despite the cut melon and peeled lemon. The earliest dated work in the corpus and the horizontal, diagonal, tipped-basket formula in its first form. Not seen as an image by this leg; no visual description beyond the museum text is offered.

## W2 -- Flowers and Still Life, c. 1650-1660

Oil on canvas, 53.3 x 73.8 cm, VERIFIED (Fitzwilliam Museum API record, object 1490, accession PD.24-1966, Broughton Collection). Seen as image (f25, 800 x 561 px). A horizontal ledge piece on a black ground. At left a small dark vase holds pink roses, a white snowball bloom and orange marigolds, with a wheat ear lying on the ledge; at right a heap of fruit, white grapes, red plums, peaches, an orange; at centre a half-peeled lemon, its peel ribbon curling down and cherries dangling over the ledge edge below it; a tall dark glass vessel stands at top right, nearly lost in the black. The flowers occupy only the left third; the fruit does the rest. The Fitzwilliam dates it 1650-1660, which would make it one of the earliest works here, and the composition already has the whole mature kit: black ground, ledge, peeled lemon, dangling cherries, wheat.

## W3 -- A Still Life of Fruit with a Melon, Grapes and Celery together with Oranges and Peaches, an Artichoke, Walnuts and Corn on a Ledge with Butterflies and Snails, a Stone Grotto and a Landscape beyond, c. 1657 (TEXT ONLY)

Oil on canvas, 64.4 x 78 cm, REPORTED (Sotheby's lot essay, 2016/2017 Old Masters sale). Signed "C. DE. HEEM. f." Sotheby's calls it a beautifully preserved early canvas and places it in a small group of exceptionally high-quality early works painted in Antwerp in the mid- to late 1650s, when Cornelis, still in his twenties, worked and studied in his father's studio. Its celery, artichoke and melon are adapted from his father's Still-life of fruit and flowers in a landscape of 1655 (Hermitage); Dr. Fred G. Meijer suggested a date around 1657 on photographs. A comparable fruit-and-vegetable arrangement recurs in the signed and dated Stadel copper of 1658 (W5). Not seen as an image by this leg; included because it is the fullest documented early canvas and because it names the grotto-and-landscape device that the later works drop. Not to be merged with W5: different support, different size, different picture (leg 2 correction 7).

## W4 -- Pageantry Still Life with Copulating Sparrows (Prunkstilleben), 1657

Oil on canvas. Stadelsches Kunstinstitut, Frankfurt. Seen as image (f01, 1920 x 1634 px). The early pronk piece, and the most crowded picture in the corpus. A wooden table at left, draped in dark green velvet with a gold-fringed cloth, holds a silver tazza heaped with figs, white grapes, a cut melon with orange flesh, pink roses, oysters on a pewter plate, lemon halves, red grapes spilling over the table edge; a nautilus shell, a pink conch and a flute lean against a framed mirror on a blue-green chest at right; a green curtain with a tassel hangs from the top; two sparrows copulate on a branch at centre right, a red admiral butterfly above them, a white moth below. The Wikipedia article names this picture as one of the two captioned works. Dated 1657: the sparrows, the mirror, the flute and the shells make it a vanitas as much as a pronk, and it is the only work here where the living animals are doing something indecent in the middle of the feast. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W5 -- Still Life with Vegetables and Fruit before a Garden Balustrade, 1658 (TEXT ONLY)

Oil on copper, 69.8 x 87.1 cm, REPORTED (Sotheby's lot essay, citing J. Sander and B. Brinkmann, Niederlandische Gemalde vor 1800 im Stadel, 1995, p. 34; Stadel inv. 721). Signed and dated 1658. A lavish display of fruits and vegetables spilling from baskets and arranged on a stone ledge, set against an architectural background with a view into a garden, with a blue-and-white Chinese porcelain pitcher among the elements (secondary descriptions). The early work that most clearly shows the father's influence, and the only dated work in the corpus on copper. Not seen as an image by this leg; included because it is the dated anchor for the early garden-background manner and the source of the 1658 date for the whole early group.

## W6 -- Still Life with a Lobster, Fruits and Flowers, 1660-1670

Oil on canvas. Gemaldegalerie Alte Meister, Dresden. Seen as image (f18, 1920 x 1428 px, photographed in its frame in the gallery). A red lobster lies at lower right, claws toward the viewer, its body the brightest red in the picture; above it red plums, peaches, white grapes spilling from a blue-and-white porcelain bowl, blue plums, blackberries and raspberries on their canes, a wheat ear, a pink rose, vine leaves; a snail sits on a twig at far left. The ground is black and the fruit heap is lit from above left. Inventory "153" lower right. The lobster-and-fruit formula at its most painterly: the fruit is heaped, not arranged, and the porcelain bowl is half-hidden by the heap, so the picture reads as abundance first and inventory second. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W7 -- Still Life with Fruit and Oysters, undated in sources read

Oil on panel. Ashmolean Museum, Oxford, WA1940.2.32. Seen as image (f16, 737 x 944 px). A small vertical niche piece: a stone ledge inside a dark arch. At left a whole orange; centre a half-peeled lemon, its peel uncoiling down over the ledge edge in a long spiral; at right three open oysters on the ledge and a split red fig; above, white grapes on the vine and rose hips; a red admiral butterfly at top left, a small fly at right. The lemon peel is the picture's spine: it starts at the lemon's cut face and hangs down past the ledge, the longest single line in the composition. The Ashmolean catalogue (Meijer 2003) is the source cited for the early Sotheby's canvas (W3); no date is assigned to this work here. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W8 -- Still-Life of Fruit, undated in sources read

Oil on canvas. Collection not verified in sources read. Seen as image (f12, 880 x 1072 px). A stone ledge inside a dark arched niche. Peaches at centre, one split open to show the stone; blue plums at left; white and red grapes heaped at right, one bunch cascading over the ledge edge; a pomegranate at right; orange physalis fruits dangling below the ledge at left; a red martagon lily curling at top left; a blue morning glory at centre right. The niche arch is visible at the top corners, framing the heap like a shrine. The same arched-niche ledge as W7, but horizontal and fruit-only, no oysters, no lemon peel. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W9 -- Still Life with a Lobster, undated in sources read

Oil on panel. National Museum in Warsaw, M.Ob.2725. Seen as image (f05, 1920 x 2352 px). A vertical, near-black picture. A whole red lobster lies on a pewter plate across the lower half, its long antennae reaching left, one claw hanging over the plate edge; behind it a huge bunch of white grapes and a dark roemer glass, the wine line just visible, its bowl nearly black against the black ground; vine tendrils curl at top right; a dark blue cloth drapes the table at lower left. The lobster is the only strong colour: everything else is green grape, black glass, black ground. The roemer is the tallest thing in the picture and the darkest, a black shape inside blackness, legible only by its rim highlight. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W10 -- Still Life with Flowers and Fruits, c. 1670 (date REPORTED, not verified)

Oil on canvas, 55 x 73 cm, REPORTED (ArtsDot/WahooArt secondary listings, consistent across sources; Museum voor Schone Kunsten, Ghent, inv. 1907-B). Dated 1670 in the meisterdrucke notable-works listing; that date is reported, not verified, and is not relied on for the evolution arc. Seen as image three times: f02 (1554 x 1183 px), f06 (1920 x 1481 px, the Ghent museum scan) and f13 (1058 x 807 px, the WGA scan) -- three photographs of the same painting; count once (leg 2 correction 1). A fruit-and-flower heap on a stone ledge against black: white grapes at left, peaches at centre, one split open, a whole orange and a half-peeled lemon with its peel ribbon at right, a red peony, pink roses, blue morning glories, a chestnut in its spiky husk at lower left, snails on the vine at left, wheat ears, blackberries and cherries dangling below the ledge, a dark glass vessel behind the heap. The most repeated composition in the corpus: the same heap, with the same split peach and the same orange, recurs in the WGA and museum scans, and the repetition is the point -- this is the formula at its most finished, fruit and flower in one heap, nothing else.

## W11 -- Still-Life with Oysters, Lemons and Grapes, undated in sources read

Oil on panel. Residenzgalerie, Salzburg. Seen as image twice: f14 (1300 x 1005 px, WGA scan) and f17 (1915 x 1509 px, the Residenzgalerie scan) -- two photographs of the same painting; count once (leg 2 correction 2). A wooden table with a dark blue fringed cloth holds a pewter plate of oysters at right; at centre a tall silver vessel with a pierced domed lid, an orange sitting on top of it; at left a half-peeled lemon, its peel ribbon looping down over the table edge; red grapes heaped behind; cherries on their stems dangling over the table edge at lower right; hazelnuts in their husks at far left; a silver caster tipped on its side behind the grapes. Signed "C. DE HEEM" on the table edge at lower left. The silver vessel is the rendering set-piece: chased, reflective, its pierced lid a field of small dark holes, the orange balanced on it like a finial. The diagonal light falls from upper left across a brown wall. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W12 -- Breakfast Still Life (Fruhstucksstilleben), undated in sources read

Oil on canvas. Kunsthistorisches Museum, Vienna, Gemaldegalerie, GG 567. Seen as image (f19, 1424 x 1144 px). A wooden table with a dark cloth: at left a silver caster with a pierced dome, a pocket watch with a blue ribbon, a knife; centre a pewter plate of three open oysters, a half-peeled lemon, red grapes, a cut melon; at right a bread roll and dark plums. Cherries dangle over the table edge at lower centre. The ground is warm brown, not black, and a diagonal shaft of light crosses the wall behind. The pocket watch is the only timepiece in the corpus seen, and it sits beside the oysters like a second vanitas note. The blue ribbon of the watch answers the blue cloth; the caster repeats W11's silver vessel in a smaller, plainer form. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W13 -- Nature morte (Still Life with Pie, Goblet and Fruit), undated in sources read

Oil on panel. Musee des Beaux-Arts, Valenciennes. Seen as image (f20, 1920 x 1504 px). A warm brown picture, the only one in the corpus with no black ground at all. At left a large silver-gilt goblet lies tipped on its side on a white napkin, its chased bowl catching the light; beside it a small gold ewer; centre a cut-open pie on a pewter plate, its dark filling exposed, a spoon lying across it; cherries scattered on the napkin; at right white grapes, a half-peeled lemon whose peel hangs in a long loop over the table edge, and a peach. Vine leaves hang from above. The tipped goblet is the disturbance: everything else in the corpus stands upright, and here the grandest object has fallen over. The pie is the only pie in the corpus. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W14 -- Still Life with a Lobster (Stillleben mit Hummer), undated in sources read

Oil on panel. Kunsthaus Zurich, KS 14. Seen as image (f22, 941 x 1200 px). A red lobster lies on a table draped in strong blue cloth with a gold fringe, its claws reaching left over the table edge; behind it a huge facon de Venise glass of white wine, its bowl catching the light, and behind that a tall thin lidded glass; at left white grapes and a knife with a black-and-white chequered handle; at right a half-peeled lemon with its peel ribbon and dark plums on a pewter plate; a few shrimps on the plate. The wall behind is pale grey-brown with a diagonal shaft of light, the lightest ground in the corpus after W13. The blue cloth is the strong-blue note at its loudest: the whole lower half of the picture is blue, and the red lobster sits on it like a seal on wax. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W15 -- Festoon with Fruit and Flowers, undated in sources read

Oil on canvas. Collection not verified in sources read. Seen as image (f11, 900 x 1130 px). No table at all: a bunch of fruit hangs from a big blue ribbon bow tied at the top, against a dark ground inside a faint arched frame. White grapes at centre, oranges at right, a peach at top left, red grapes below, medlars at the bottom, vine leaves, and at far left a single red lobster claw. The bow is the picture's top and its only manufactured object: everything else hangs from it. The fruit is arranged by weight, the heaviest bunches lowest, the grapes in the middle, the peach at the top -- gravity does the composition. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W16 -- Garland with Flowers and Fruit (Guirlande met bloemen en vruchten), undated in sources read

Oil on canvas. Collection not verified in sources read; Rijksmuseum holds a garland of this title. Seen as image (f21, 1200 x 964 px). A horizontal garland hung from two blue ribbon bows, one at each top corner, against a dark brown ground. Pink and white roses at centre, red and white grapes, oranges, chestnuts in their husks, an apple, a pomegranate, blue morning glories, wheat; butterflies and moths at the top, centre and bottom -- a brown butterfly at top centre, a white moth at centre right, a white butterfly at lower left, a red admiral at bottom centre. Signed "C. DE HEEM" lower right. The garland is the festoon doubled: two bows, two ends, the fruit sagging in the middle like a swag. The insects are the only movement, and there are four of them. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W17 -- Vanitas Flower Still Life, undated in sources read

Oil on canvas. Collection not verified in sources read. Seen as image (f09, 1920 x 2315 px). The outdoor exception. A huge bouquet -- white lilies, orange poppies, pink and red roses, white snowballs, red carnations, a sunflower, blue morning glories -- stands in a stone niche outdoors: a blue sky with clouds at top left, a tree trunk at right, a brick wall at left, the ground at the bottom. At the foot of the bouquet: a cut melon, peaches, mushrooms, a lizard at far left, a snail and a slug at centre, a butterfly at top left. Signed "C. DE. HEEM" on the stone at right. Nothing here is on a table and nothing is indoors; the flowers are in the open air and the decay is literal -- mushrooms, a lizard, a slug eating at the base. It is the only work in the corpus where the vanitas is the setting, not a note. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W18 -- Floral Still Life, undated in sources read

Oil on canvas. Minneapolis Institute of Art, 2016.33.27. Seen as image (f07, 1920 x 2619 px). A vertical flower piece on a grey-brown ground, the lightest ground of any flower piece here. A dark glass vase holds four large pink roses, buds, columbine and a spray of white cow parsley; on the ledge at left a peeled lemon with its peel spiral and a whole lemon; at right a snail on the ledge; a red wax seal and a torn scrap of paper at the far left edge; a small spider at far right. The roses are the largest single blooms in the corpus, each the size of a fist, and there are only four of them: this is the sparsest bouquet in the corpus, four roses and a vase, where W10 heaps a dozen kinds of fruit and flower. The wax seal and torn paper are trompe-l'oeil notes, the picture pretending to be a letter or a document. Dimensions not verified in sources read (the Minneapolis page was not read).

## W19 -- Still Life with Fruit, undated in sources read

Oil on panel. Collection not verified in sources read. Seen as image (f03, 1900 x 1268 px). A pewter plate on a dark ledge holds two bunches of grapes -- green at left, pale gold at right -- and three peaches; below the plate, on the ledge, three red cherries; at lower right a single peach hangs over the ledge edge on its leafy stem, its leaves with holes eaten in them. Dark grapes hang in the black above. The picture is almost a diagram of the grape: two bunches, two colours, each grape a separate translucent sphere with its own highlight, the green bunch lit from the left and the gold bunch from the right. The holed leaves are the small damage that makes the fruit look picked, not arranged. Dimensions not verified in sources read.

## W20 -- Fruit Still Life, 1695

Oil on canvas, 65 x 50 cm, REPORTED (Mauritshuis 1910 abridged catalogue: "Fruit. 50. On canvas, 0.65 by 0.50. Signed: C. DE HEEM."; the 1695 date and the 65 x 50 cm size are also given in the niceartgallery survey; the Mauritshuis collection page itself was not read end to end, so this is not counted as verified). Mauritshuis, The Hague, inv. 50. Seen as image (f04, 1920 x 2443 px). A vertical piece inside a dark arched niche: a stone ledge at the bottom holds a heap of fruit -- split peach at left, whole peaches, green grapes, dark grapes, pomegranates, chestnuts in their husks, acorns, oranges, one half-peeled with its peel ribbon -- and the heap rises in a diagonal from lower left to upper right. A snail sits on the peel at lower right; a white moth rests on a peach at the bottom; wheat ears lie on the ledge. Signed "C. DE HEEM" on the ledge at lower left. Painted in the year of his death, and the most vertical, most heaped, most diagonal picture in the corpus: the fruit is stacked, not laid out, and the top half of the panel is empty dark niche. Dimensions reported, not verified.

## Beyond the 20

Works seen as images but not numbered, all in ~/workspace/fly-watch/560-deheem/:

- Still Life -- Apples and Grapes, National Museum in Warsaw, M.Ob.2539 (f08, 1920 x 2296 px). A vertical, very dark picture: red apples and peaches heaped at centre, white grapes cascading over the ledge edge, cherries dangling below the ledge, a dark jug at right, one apple floating on a bare twig at top right. The cascade over the edge is the longest in the corpus.
- Putti Crowning a Chalice (f10, 780 x 1042 px). Two putti hold a wreath of pink and white roses over a silver chalice; fruit heaps fill the top corners; a fly sits on the right putto's back. The only work in the corpus with figures, and the only devotional picture: the chalice is crowned, not filled.
- Still Life with Peeled Lemon and Red Crab (f15, 590 x 777 px). Seen ONLY as a black-and-white reproduction, very dark; the lemon peel spiral and a bunch of grapes are legible, the crab is barely so. No colour claims.
- Fruit Still Life with Frogs, Bavarian State Painting Collections, inv. 6335 (f23, 614 x 768 px). Seen ONLY as a black-and-white reproduction; a stone ledge with a cut melon, grapes, peaches, mushrooms and a tree trunk, sky at top left. The frogs of the title are not clearly identifiable in this reproduction, so nothing is claimed about them beyond the title.
- Still Life with Fruit (f24, 1920 x 1430 px). A horizontal dark-ground piece: a blue-and-white porcelain bowl heaped with plums and blackberries at right, white grapes at left, a half-peeled lemon at centre whose peel hangs in a long loop over the ledge edge, red plums, peaches and cherries. The peel loop is the longest single ribbon in the corpus.

Works touched in search but not numbered, all text-only for this leg:

- Still Life with Fruit on a Ledge, New Orleans Museum of Art, 83.50. Oil on canvas, 26 1/2 x 21 1/2 in. (67.3 x 54.6 cm), VERIFIED (NOMA object lesson, read end to end, 43 lines). The museum's reading: orange peels and wheat stalks spill over the ledge, "lessening the gap between the pictorial space and the viewer's space"; the lemon is "inviting on the outside, but sour on the inside"; the oyster a symbol of lust, the butterfly the soul and its redemption, the grapes and wheat the Eucharist; the perfectly ripe fruit a vanitas, "alluding to the erosive character of time". Not seen as an image by this leg.
- Vanitas Still Life with Musical Instruments, 1661 (niceartgallery survey). The only dated vanitas in the corpus; not seen as an image by this leg.
- A Swag of Fruit and Flowers Hanging in a Casement, late 17th century, attributed to Cornelis de Heem, Menil Collection, 1982-09 DJ. Oil on canvas, 68.3 x 54.6 cm (Menil collection record). A third hanging piece, attributed not signed; not seen as an image by this leg.
- Still Life of Flowers and Fruit Arranged on a Stone Plinth in a Garden, c. 1685, National Trust. Oil on canvas, 92 x 77 cm (Art Fund record). Painted when he was living in The Hague; the largest reported canvas in the corpus and the late garden piece; not seen as an image by this leg.
- Grapes, a Tulip, Morning Glory, Medlars, Plums, Chestnuts, a Pomegranate, a White and a Pink Rose, Peaches, Snowdrops, a Butterfly, a Snail and a Grasshopper on a Ledge in a Niche, Christie's lot record. Oil on canvas, 53.5 x 45.5 cm, signed lower right "C. De Heem f". The fullest single inventory of the ledge-in-a-niche formula; not seen as an image by this leg.
- Fruit pendants 874C and Still Life with Fruits 874A, Gemaldegalerie Berlin; Still Life with Fruit and Oysters (follower), Still Life with Grapes, Peaches and a Bohemian Glass Goblet, Still Life with Pewter Plate and Fruits (National Trust 1298187), Still Life with Fruit and Oysters (Glasgow, attributed), Flower Piece (Portsmouth, attributed), Fruit and Flowers (V and A, attributed) -- files in the Commons category list, not downloaded.

## Evolution

Early (c. 1654-1658): the father's idiom, closely followed. W1 (c. 1654) is a horizontal diagonal on a green cloth against a dark green wall, the basket tipped, the melon cut, the palette green-based (NMWA). W2 (c. 1650-1660) already has the black ground and the ledge, but the flowers still take a third of the picture. W3 (c. 1657) and W5 (1658) open onto landscape: a grotto, a balustrade, a garden, a blue-and-white porcelain pitcher, the father's motifs (celery, artichoke, melon) freely rewoven. W4 (1657) is the early pronk, the most crowded picture in the corpus, with a curtain, a mirror, a flute and copulating sparrows. The early works are on panel and on copper, signed on the table edge, and they are the only ones with a background that is a place.

Middle (c. 1660-1670s): the formula closes in. The background goes black or warm brown; the ledge becomes stone; the niche appears (W7, W8); the lobster arrives as the red anchor (W6, W9); the silver vessel is repeated (W11, W12); the festoon and the garland leave the table behind entirely (W15, W16); the blue cloth and the blue ribbon harden into the strong-blue signature (W14). The heap gets denser and the objects fewer in kind: fruit, one vessel, one lobster, one lemon, one snail. The father's painterliness is the thing that leaves: the handling gets harder, cooler, more exact, the grapes more separate, the highlights more distinct -- and the brushstroke itself gets swifter and easier from the 1670s (leg 1 F11).

Late (c. 1685-1695): the vertical niche and the tall heap. W20 (1695) stacks the fruit in a diagonal from lower left to upper right inside a tall dark arch, the top half of the panel empty; the Art Fund plinth piece (c. 1685, 92 x 77 cm) puts the flowers and fruit on a stone plinth in a garden, the largest reported canvas, a return to the outdoor setting at a bigger scale; W18 (Minneapolis) pares the bouquet down to four roses in a glass vase, the sparsest picture in the corpus. The late manner is not a decline but a sharpening: fewer kinds of thing, each rendered harder, the composition taller and emptier at the top.

What does not change: the grapes, the peaches, the peeled lemon, the stone ledge, the signature carved on the stone, the snail, the black ground. What changes: the background (garden to black to niche), the format (horizontal early, vertical late), the handling (painterly to hard and exact, then swift), and the blue (from an accent to a signature). The arc is from the father's garden to the son's cabinet: the same abundance, folded smaller, bluer, and harder.

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

2 of 20 verified on a museum page read end to end or a museum API record: W1 (NMWA Tokyo P.1990-0002, 44.5 x 72.5 cm, oil on panel), W2 (Fitzwilliam PD.24-1966, 53.3 x 73.8 cm, oil on canvas, Fitzwilliam API record). A third verified set belongs to a text-only work in Beyond the 20: NOMA Still Life with Fruit on a Ledge, 26 1/2 x 21 1/2 in. (67.3 x 54.6 cm), NOMA page read end to end. REPORTED, not counted as verified: W3 (Sotheby's lot essay, 64.4 x 78 cm), W5 (Stadel 1658, 69.8 x 87.1 cm, Sotheby's essay citing Sander/Brinkmann), W10 (Ghent MSK 1907-B, 55 x 73 cm, ArtsDot/WahooArt secondary listings), W20 (Mauritshuis 50, 65 x 50 cm, 1910 Mauritshuis abridged catalogue). NOT VERIFIED: W4, W6, W7, W8, W9, W11, W12, W13, W14, W15, W16, W17, W18, W19.

## Aesthetic logic

The picture is a cabinet, not a table. Everything in Cornelis de Heem's mature work serves one decision: take the abundance his father spread across a banquet and fold it into a small, hard, blue-lit box. The black ground is not emptiness but a wall with a niche cut into it; the stone ledge is the shelf; the heap is the display. The fruit is life-size because the panel is arm's length -- the viewer does not survey the picture, they lean into it. The blue is the cold note that keeps the warm heap from going muddy, and it is carried by the manufactured things (cloth, ribbon, porcelain) so the fruit itself stays warm. The lobster is the red anchor that stops the blue from going cold. The grapes are the lamp: the only light in the picture that comes from inside the heap. The peel is the one line that leaves the picture, falling over the edge toward the viewer, and the signature is carved into the stone it falls past -- the painter signing the furniture, not the picture. The living intruder is the only thing that moves, and it moves slowly: a snail, a butterfly, a moth. The vanitas is carried by the fruit itself, ripe and cut and about to spoil, not by a skull. The handling follows the same narrowing as the format: early pictures are tight and father-close, late pictures are swift and easy, the brushstroke loosening as the composition hardens. The son's cabinet is the father's banquet after the move north: smaller, bluer, harder, and entirely his own.

## Positions

1. The workshop was not inherited. No source says Cornelis inherited or took over any workshop; the phrase was coined inside the chain in #557 leg 3 and carried twice. The father's Utrecht workshop went to Mignon in 1672; Cornelis worked self-employed in the north from 1667. Any piece built on "the son who inherited the workshop" is built on a chain invention, not a fact.
2. Guild member in 1660, not Guild master. Wikipedia says "member", Sotheby's and Ketterer say "as a painter in his own right" / "independent painter". No source read calls him master in 1660.
3. "Jewel-like" is the chain's gloss, not a source word. The smallness is sourced (Wikipedia: works "tend to be small"), the adjective is not. Pieces may call the pictures small and dense; they must not present "jewel-like" as a sourced description.
4. The blue is the diagnostic, not the whole palette. The early pictures are green-based and lighter; the strong blue and the dark ground are the mature manner. A piece that paints only blue misses the arc from green to dark.
5. Symbolism is switchable, not universal. The NOMA reading (lemon, oyster, butterfly, grapes, wheat) is the museum's, and the NMWA says the early Basket of Fruit "seems to not have any specifically symbolic meaning". Pieces may use the symbolic notes; they must not load every fruit with a meaning.
6. The late chronology is open. The 1695 Mauritshuis date and the 1670 Ghent date are reported, not verified; the last reliably dated work is 1659 (Musee Fabre, signed and dated) with 1658 the last dated anchor on copper. The gap after the 1660s stays visible in the study and in any piece built on the late works.
7. The Rijksmuseum Vanitas Still-Life with Musical Instruments is contested. Secondary sources attribute the same-titled c. 1660s picture to Cornelis and to Jan Davidsz. alike, and the Rijksmuseum page was not read this leg. Do not cite it as Cornelis's without verifying the Rijksmuseum object record (leg 1 C726).

## Quotes

- "Cornelis's works, however, tend to be small, display a preference for strong blues, and, over time, shifted away from the painterly style preferred by his father." -- EN Wikipedia, Cornelis de Heem.
- "In his formative years Cornelis de Heem was trained in the studio of his father Jan Davidsz. de Heem (1606-1683/4) in Antwerp." -- Sotheby's, lot 181.
- "With realistic details, Cornelis de Heem attains a surprisingly easy and swift stroke of the brush, a feature that became more manifest as of the 1670s." -- Ketterer Kunst.
- "His still lives, mostly executed on canvas, were made in smaller format and seem a bit more coarse in their execution." -- Ketterer Kunst.
- "The complex overlapping arrangement and consistent tonal quality produce an emphatically unified composition." -- NOMA, Still Life with Fruit on a Ledge.
- "Orange peels and wheat stalks spill over the ledge, lessening the gap between the pictorial space and the viewer's space, and thus beckoning the viewer to contemplate the significance of the message." -- NOMA, Still Life with Fruit on a Ledge.
- "The blue of the plums and the porcelain bowl have bloomed a little." -- Sotheby's, lot 181, condition note.
- "The overall palette is green-based, with accents found in the yellow lemon and the red cherries." -- National Museum of Western Art, Tokyo, Still Life with a Basket of Fruit.
- "Such works often had vanitas meanings during that period, this work seems to not have any specifically symbolic meaning." -- National Museum of Western Art, Tokyo, Still Life with a Basket of Fruit.
- "It is often not easy to distinguish the works of the different members of the family." -- EN Wikipedia, Cornelis de Heem.

## Network

1. Father and teacher (N1-N4). Jan Davidsz. de Heem (bapt. 17 April 1606 - before 26 April 1684) -- the father whose Antwerp studio trained Cornelis and whose Utrecht workshop went to Mignon, not to the son. Cornelis was baptised in Leiden on 8 April 1631; the family moved to Antwerp in 1636. Wikipedia hedges the training ("He appears to have been trained by his father in Antwerp"); Sotheby's and Meijer do not. The father's own teacher is the hedged link in the chain: Balthasar van der Ast "probably" taught Jan Davidsz., no document (see D4). The father's pupils are the workshop Cornelis grew up in: Michiel Verstylen, Alexander Coosemans, Thomas de Klerck, Lenaert Rougghe, Theodor Aenvanck, Andries Benedetti, Elias van den Broeck, Jacob Marrel, Hendrik Schoock, Abraham Mignon (#557 file L21). Coosemans and Broeck are carried Mains/Reserve; Marrel and Mignon are carried in the #556 block.

2. The family and the hand question (N5-N7). Jan Jansz. de Heem (bapt. 2 July 1650 - after 1695), the other son, trained in Utrecht 1667-1672 -- FLAG: Jan Jansz. as a painter is most probably a myth per Meijer; his unsigned work is still being peeled off his father's oeuvre. He stays Reserve. David Cornelisz de Heem (1663-1701 vs 1663-1718 / after 1718) -- the son, whose death date conflicts; Sotheby's says father and son "most probably shared a studio in The Hague around c. 1685-90", with works by apparently the same hand signed "C. de Heem" one time and "D. de Heem" the other. HELD -- too thin while the dates conflict. No documented non-family pupils were found in any source read; no documented non-family collaboration; no named patrons were found in any source read. Recorded as not established, not as absence. The wider de Heem family is held: David de Heem the Elder (1570-1631/32; Getty ULAN: "musician, died in 1612") -- musician-vs-painter and death-date conflict, no surviving oeuvre; David Davidsz. de Heem (1610-after 1669) -- Jan's younger brother, Cornelis's uncle, also a painter (NEW Spare, thin, no works cited); Jan or Johannes de Heem (c. 1603-probably after 1659) -- cousin, painter of still lifes, flowers and fruits (NEW Spare, thin, no works cited); Jan Jansz. II de Heem -- Getty ULAN date conflicts, no standalone record, held.

3. The Antwerp circle (N9-N10). Nicolaes van Verendael (bapt. 19 February 1640 - 11 August 1691) -- the collaborator: a de Heem painting "reworked by Nicolaes van Verendael", "signed by both artists" (Sotheby's; Munich, Alte Pinakothek inv. 568); V and A: "with whom he collaborated on a flower still-life (Munich, Alte Pin.)". His wider collaborator circle per Wikipedia: David Teniers the Younger, Gonzales Coques, Erasmus Quellinus II, Jan Boeckhorst, Carstian Luyckx and Jan Davidsz. de Heem. Trained with his father Willem van Veerendael; admitted to the Antwerp Guild as the son of a member in 1657. Teacher of 'priest Cano' and of the flower painter Jean Baptiste Morel. Dated works 1659-1690. FLAG: V and A gives "1626-1691" against the 19 February 1640 baptism; the V and A date is the outlier. TOP PICK for #561. Alexander Coosemans (bapt. 18 March 1627 - 28 October 1689) -- the pupil of 1641, master at 18, Rome 1649-1651, whose crowded still lifes are more varied than his master's; the only dated work is after de Heem. Carried Reserve. Joris van Son (bapt. 24 September 1623 - buried 25 June 1667) -- the Antwerp fruit man, de Heem's softest-lit heir; fish still lifes for the Four Elements, garlands with figure painters, dead at 44. Carried Reserve. FLAG: pupil status hedged, not documented.

4. The Amsterdam return (N9, N11-N13). Elias van den Broeck (c. 1649/1652 - buried 6 February 1708) -- the pupil who followed de Heem from Utrecht to Antwerp in 1673 and took the pronk still life back to Amsterdam; dead-colouring and orpiment, per the SMK technical study. Pupil of Cornelis Kick from August 1665; pupil of Jan Davidsz. de Heem in Utrecht 1669; master in the Antwerp Guild 1673; returned to Amsterdam 1685. FLAG: birth 1649 vs c. 1652; Houbraken "died 1711" against the 1708 burial. Carried Main; strong alternate for #561, better saved for #562 (the Amsterdam return, with Kick, Kouwenbergh and Walscapelle in tow). Cornelis Kick (bapt. 12 March 1634 - 18 June 1681) -- Broeck's first teacher (1665-1669) and Walscapelle's teacher (1664-1667); trained under his father Simon Kick; Prabook/Invaluable say he was trained by / in the manner of Jan Davidsz. de Heem; per Houbraken, he painted flowers from nature in his father-in-law's garden with his pupil Jacob van Walscapelle. Paintings rare, often mistaken for Walscapelle's (Sotheby's). NEW Alternate. FLAG: birth year 1631 vs 1634 vs 1635 (leg 3 C745). Jacob van Walscapelle (1644, Dordrecht - 16 August 1727, Amsterdam) -- Kick's pupil 1664-1667; "painted fruit and flower still lifes in the manner of Jan Davidsz de Heem, and many of his works have been confused with those of Kick" (Wikipedia, RKD); National Gallery, London: Flowers in a Glass Vase, c. 1670. NEW Spare. Philip van Kouwenbergh (1671-1729) -- Amsterdam flower painter; RKD: pupil of Elias van den Broeck; Flowers in a Vase, c. 1700, National Gallery of Art, Washington. NEW Spare.

5. The French turn (N14). Jean-Baptiste Monnoyer (12 January 1636 - 20 February 1699) -- began in Antwerp as a student of Davidsz. de Heem (Sotheby's); in Paris by 1650; attached to the Gobelins and Beauvais tapestry workshops (cartoons of fruit and flowers); decorative painting at Marly, Meudon, Vincennes, Saint-Cloud, Versailles and the Grand Trianon; received at the Academie Royale in 1665; in 1690 left France for England to decorate Montagu House, Bloomsbury (over fifty panels of fruit and flowers); died in London 1699. The French turn of the de Heem line. NEW Alternate. FLAGS: birth 19 July 1634 (Dictionary of National Biography) vs 12 January 1636 (Wikipedia); the de Heem pupillage rests on Sotheby's alone and is squeezed by "in Paris by 1650" (leg 3 C746).

6. The garland forward (N10, N15). Jan Baptist Morel (bapt. 26 October 1662, Antwerp - believed died Brussels 1732) -- Verendael's pupil c. 1674; became a member of the Brussels Guild of Saint Luke in 1699; summoned to Brussels by governor Maximilian II Emanuel; recorded back in Brussels in 1729. His garland paintings go back to the genre invented by Jan Brueghel the Elder and systematised by Daniel Seghers. NEW Spare. FLAG: lot-art gives a Brussels Guild date of 1669, impossible at age 7; 1699 is the anchored date (leg 3 C747).

7. The Leiden latecomer (N17). Jan Mortel (fl. 1675-1716) -- Leiden still-life painter; pupil of Jan Porcellis van Delden; "His style was much influenced by Jan Davidsz. de Heem and Abraham Mignon, whom Mortel emulated late in his career" (Sotheby's); from 1690 official painter of the University of Leiden's botanical gardens; dated compositions range from 1675 to 1716; signed and dated work JMortel fecit / 1715. Birth and death dates not established in any source read -- proposed on the dated-works range only. NEW Spare.

8. Adjacent artists -- why they matter for the chain (leg 3). Nicolaes van Verendael -- the collaborator hop, and the man in whom the last three studies meet: he collaborated with Jan Davidsz. de Heem (#557), he carries Daniel Seghers's garland system (#558), and he was Cornelis de Heem's Antwerp contemporary (#560). His pupil Morel carries the garland on to Brussels and the 18th century. Elias van den Broeck -- the pupil hop, and the Amsterdam return: after Verendael closes the Antwerp arc, Broeck opens Amsterdam with Kick, Kouwenbergh and Walscapelle in tow. Cornelis Kick -- the teacher between the two: Broeck's first teacher and Walscapelle's, the Amsterdam flower painter who taught the pupil who carried the pronk still life back to Amsterdam. All three are carried as candidates -- Verendael and Broeck as Mains, Kick as a new Alternate -- so this is adjacent-artist analysis, not new proposals beyond the eight named in section 4 of leg 3.

## Future candidates

Top pick for #561 is Nicolaes van Verendael (bapt. 19 February 1640 - 11 August 1691), carried from #557 through #558 and #559, and named as the other strong hop in the #559 entry: "Why Cornelis over Nicolaes van Verendael and Elias van den Broeck (the other two Mains): Verendael is the collaborator and Broeck the pupil who carried the pronk still life back to Amsterdam". Cornelis is taken now, so he is. The chain hop is the collaborator who worked with de Heem, Teniers, Coques and Quellinus; Seghers's garland loosened into proto-Rococo grace, then darkened by de Heem. From Cornelis -- the son trained in his father's Antwerp studio, member of the Antwerp Guild in 1660, who painted the fruit piece small, blue and hard -- the tightest next move is the man who literally reworked a de Heem painting and signed it with him: a de Heem painting "reworked by Nicolaes van Verendael", "signed by both artists" (Sotheby's; Munich, Alte Pinakothek inv. 568). Verendael closes the Antwerp arc: he collaborated with Jan Davidsz. de Heem (#557), he carries Daniel Seghers's garland system (#558), and he was Cornelis de Heem's Antwerp contemporary (#560). All three of the last studies meet in him. His dated corpus runs 1659-1690 (flower pieces, vanitas still lifes, a few monkey allegories), and his pupil Jan Baptist Morel carries the garland on to Brussels and the 18th century, so the study has a forward hop as well as a backward one. Why Verendael over Elias van den Broeck (the other Main): Broeck is the strong alternate, but his Amsterdam return is the bigger geographic move and the better #562: after Verendael closes the Antwerp arc, Broeck opens Amsterdam with Kick, Kouwenbergh and Walscapelle in tow. Broeck also carries two date flags (birth 1649 vs c. 1652; death 1708 burial vs Houbraken 1711) while Verendael's record is cleaner (one flag). Why Verendael over Jan Jansz. de Heem and Alexander Coosemans (the Reserves): Jan Jansz. stays Reserve because the attribution question (Meijer: most probably a myth) is the study of an attribution, not of a painter, and Cornelis already holds the family slot as #560; Coosemans is a pupil, not a collaborator -- the collaborator hop is the tighter chain move from Cornelis -- and his "only dated work is after de Heem" makes him thinner. Dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in #557 leg 3 (130 lines, leg 1 P4 this run). FLAG (carried #557 F6): V&A gives "1626-1691" against the 19 February 1640 baptism; the V&A date is the outlier. FLAG (leg 1 C724): the death day-month, 11 August, is carried from #559; Wikipedia gives only the year 1691 -- the day-month needs a source check.

The study of the garland as connoisseurship -- the man who catalogued the collection that collected Seghers. Juan de Arellano (3 August 1614 - 13 October 1676) -- the Madrid heir: Seghers's garland carried across the confessional and linguistic divide into Spain, then loosened toward Mario Nuzzi and the Baroque basket; the 1646 Camilo garland, the 1652 Prado landscapes in stone cartouches. The study of how a Flemish system becomes Spanish. 2 whole-log mentions (was 1 at #559's writing; the #559 entry added 1). Ignace Raeth (c. 1626-1666) -- the thinnest record in the ranking, placed last among the Alternates for that reason: Seghers's named pupil, then a Madrid allegorist (Allegory of the Arts and Sciences, 1649, Prado). The study would be short and honest about its gaps -- or the parent may swap him for a carried Spare. "Painted fruit and flower still lifes in the manner of Jan Davidsz de Heem, and many of his works have been confused with those of Kick" (Wikipedia, RKD); V and A: "his early flower paintings can barely be discerned from his teacher's". Dated works 1667-1685 (plus a recently-surfaced example dated 1699, per the Web Gallery of Art). Influenced Michel Nicolas Micheux. 0 whole-log mentions.
